UPSC CDS English previous year questions with answers

Every English question asked in the UPSC CDS papers on this site, grouped by topic. Answers come from UPSC's official answer keys (latest papers: answers by Learn with Anil, as UPSC's key is not out yet); each has a short explanation (free here for a sample, all of them in the practice app).

Spotting Errors

  1. CDS II 2026 · English · Q41 · English · Spotting Errors

    Directions: Each item in this section has a sentence with three underlined parts labelled (a), (b) and (c). Read each sentence to determine whether there is an error in any underlined part and indicate your response on the answer sheet against the above-mentioned corresponding letter. If there is no error, indicate your response as (d). The teacher asked that who was absent in the class that day.

    1. The teacher asked
    2. that who was absent
    3. in the class that day.
    4. No error.
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) that who was absent

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

    Directions: In the following items similar sounding words are given, followed by sentences wherein in each sentence one of these words has been used, and underlined. The volunteer corps was organising a blood donation camp. (underlined: corps) 3. He was sleeping like a corpse. (underlined: corpse) In which of the sentences given above has / have the word(s) been used correctly?

    1. 2 and 3 only
    2. 2 only
    3. 1 and 3 only
    4. 1, 2 and 3
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) 1, 2 and 3

    1. 1 and 2 only
    2. 2 only
    3. 1 only
    4. 1, 2 and 3

Synonyms & Antonyms

  1. CDS II 2026 · English · Q1 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Presumptive and Presumptuous

    1. Presumptive means something likely to be true, and Presumptuous means to be disrespectfully bold
    2. Presumptive means to be disrespectfully bold, and Presumptuous means something likely to be true
    3. Presumptive means to exist, and Presumptuous means something very important
    4. Presumptive means something very important, and Presumptuous means to exist
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Presumptive means something likely to be true, and Presumptuous means to be disrespectfully bold

    "Presumptive" means based on a reasonable assumption, i.e. probably true (e.g. heir presumptive); "presumptuous" means overstepping limits, impertinently bold.

  2. CDS II 2026 · English · Q2 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Tempestuous and Temperamental

    1. Tempestuous means unpredictable behaviour, and Temperamental means displaying violent behaviour
    2. Tempestuous means displaying violent behaviour, and Temperamental means unpredictable behaviour
    3. Tempestuous means transitory, and Temperamental means determined by temperature
    4. Tempestuous means determined by temperature, and Temperamental means transitory
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Tempestuous means displaying violent behaviour, and Temperamental means unpredictable behaviour

    "Tempestuous" (from tempest, a storm) means stormy, marked by violent emotion; "temperamental" means liable to unpredictable changes of mood or behaviour.

  3. CDS II 2026 · English · Q3 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Voyager and Voyeur

    1. Voyager means a travel writer, and Voyeur means an emotionally vulnerable person
    2. Voyager means an emotionally vulnerable person, and Voyeur means a travel writer
    3. Voyager means a person who enjoys watching the private life of others, and Voyeur means a traveller
    4. Voyager means a traveller, and Voyeur means a person who enjoys watching the private life of others
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Voyager means a traveller, and Voyeur means a person who enjoys watching the private life of others

    A "voyager" is one who makes a voyage, a traveller; a "voyeur" is a person who gets pleasure from watching others' private lives; option (3) swaps the two.

  4. CDS II 2026 · English · Q4 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Differ and Defer

    1. Differ means to diffuse, and Defer means to be respectful
    2. Differ means to be respectful, and Defer means to diffuse
    3. Differ means to be different from something, and Defer means to delay action
    4. Differ means to delay action, and Defer means to be different from something
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Differ means to be different from something, and Defer means to delay action

    To "differ" is to be unlike or disagree; to "defer" is to postpone (it can also mean to yield respectfully, but only option (3) gets both words right).

  5. CDS II 2026 · English · Q5 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Elegy and Eulogy

    1. Elegy means a mournful poem, and Eulogy means a tribute to the dead
    2. Elegy means a tribute to the dead, and Eulogy means a mournful poem
    3. Elegy means an inscription on a gravestone, and Eulogy means a contemptuous utterance
    4. Elegy means an allegation, and Eulogy means an inscription on the gravestone
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Elegy means a mournful poem, and Eulogy means a tribute to the dead

    An "elegy" is a poem of mourning or lament, while a "eulogy" is a speech or writing praising someone, typically one who has just died; a gravestone inscription is an "epitaph".

  6. CDS II 2026 · English · Q6 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Concord and Concourse

    1. Concord means unity and harmony, and Concourse means the large open space in a building for people to gather
    2. Concord means convergence, and Concourse means to move jointly
    3. Concord means collaboration, and Concourse means goodwill
    4. Concord means to cohere, and Concourse means to unify diverse positions
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Concord means unity and harmony, and Concourse means the large open space in a building for people to gather

    "Concord" is agreement or harmony; a "concourse" is a large open area inside a public building (such as a station or airport) where people gather or pass through.

  7. CDS II 2026 · English · Q7 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Edify and Educe

    1. Edify means to be elated, and Educe means to subtract
    2. Edify means to improve and uplift, and Educe means to extract information
    3. Edify means to enlighten, and Educe means to reduce
    4. Edify means to delight, and Educe means to be tolerant
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Edify means to improve and uplift, and Educe means to extract information

    To "edify" is to instruct or improve someone morally or intellectually; to "educe" is to bring out or draw forth (e.g. information or a conclusion) from something. "Educe" does not mean reduce, so (3) fails.

  8. CDS II 2026 · English · Q8 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Flaunt and Flinch

    1. Flaunt means to be flamboyant, and Flinch means to be impertinent
    2. Flaunt means to strut about, and Flinch means to boast
    3. Flaunt means to display with flamboyance, and Flinch means to shy away
    4. Flaunt means to exhibit, and Flinch means to breach
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Flaunt means to display with flamboyance, and Flinch means to shy away

    To "flaunt" is to display something ostentatiously; to "flinch" is to draw back or shy away from something out of fear or pain.

  9. CDS II 2026 · English · Q9 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Ingenious and Ingenuous

    1. Ingenious means to be resourceful, and Ingenuous means to be rooted
    2. Ingenious means to be artless, and Ingenuous means to be inventive
    3. Ingenious means to be naturally talented, and Ingenuous means to be genuine
    4. Ingenious means to be creative, and Ingenuous means to be naively trusting
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Ingenious means to be creative, and Ingenuous means to be naively trusting

    "Ingenious" means clever, original and inventive; "ingenuous" means innocent, unsuspecting and naively trusting. Option (2) reverses them.

  10. CDS II 2026 · English · Q10 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Nefarious and Nebulous

    1. Nefarious means scandalous, and Nebulous means indistinct
    2. Nefarious means destitute, and Nebulous means vague
    3. Nefarious means wicked, and Nebulous means unpleasant
    4. Nefarious means fastidious, and Nebulous means exposed
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Nefarious means scandalous, and Nebulous means indistinct

    "Nebulous" means hazy, vague or indistinct, which rules out (3) "unpleasant"; "nefarious" means wicked or villainous, and of the remaining options only "scandalous" is close to that sense ("destitute" and "fastidious" are wrong).

  61. CDS I 2026 · English · Q1 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Pontoon and Platoon

    1. Pontoon means a wooden platform and Platoon means a bamboo structure
    2. Pontoon means a group of soldiers and Platoon means a series of bunkers
    3. Pontoon means a temporary bridge and Platoon means a group of soldiers
    4. Pontoon means a group of people and Platoon means a temporary bridge
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Pontoon means a temporary bridge and Platoon means a group of soldiers

    A pontoon is a flat-bottomed boat or float used to support a temporary (floating) bridge, while a platoon is a sub-unit of soldiers in a company.

  62. CDS I 2026 · English · Q2 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Invoke and Revoke

    1. Invoke means to invite trouble for doing something and Revoke means to repay debts
    2. Invoke means to use the law to achieve something and Revoke means to officially cancel permission or agreement
    3. Invoke means to use the law to achieve something and Revoke means to ignore legal procedures
    4. Invoke means to usher newness and Revoke means to stick to tradition
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Invoke means to use the law to achieve something and Revoke means to officially cancel permission or agreement

    To invoke is to call upon a law or authority in support of an action (e.g. invoke Article 356); to revoke is to officially cancel or withdraw a permission, licence or agreement.

  63. CDS I 2026 · English · Q3 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Imminent and Eminent

    1. Imminent means immobile and Eminent means extremely large
    2. Imminent means extremely large and Eminent means immobile
    3. Imminent means famous and respected, and Eminent means happening soon
    4. Imminent means happening soon and Eminent means famous and respected
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Imminent means happening soon and Eminent means famous and respected

    "Imminent" means about to happen (an imminent storm); "eminent" means famous and respected (an eminent scientist). Option (3) swaps the two meanings.

    3. Liable means the willingness to understand and Libel means being generous
    4. Liable means being generous and Libel means the willingness to understand
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Liable means being legally responsible and Libel means publishing something that is false

    "Liable" means legally responsible (liable for damages); "libel" is the publication of a false statement that damages a person's reputation.

  69. CDS I 2026 · English · Q9 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Directions: In the following items, a pair of words is provided. You are required to select the option that most appropriately describes the meaning of both the words and mark your response on the Answer Sheet accordingly. Hard and Hardly

    1. Hard means difficult to a great degree and Hardly means scarcely
    2. Hard means difficult and Hardly means very difficult
    3. Hard means with great force and Hardly means ever so often
    4. Hard means to a great degree and Hardly means never
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Hard means difficult to a great degree and Hardly means scarcely

    "Hard" means difficult or done with great effort/degree, whereas "hardly" is not its adverb in that sense: it means scarcely or barely (I could hardly see). "Hardly" does not mean "never".

  206. CDS I 2023 · English · Q120 · English · Synonyms & Antonyms

    Passage: Plastic is an essential commodity with multiple uses based on its key qualities of malleability, flexibility, and durability. Plastics are omnipresent in agriculture, fisheries, renewable energy, transport, technology, retail, textiles, personal care products, and all the other sectors and industries that directly or indirectly affect our daily life. Plastic has indeed made our lives more convenient, but it has come at a higher price than we imagined. The plastic pollution overflowing our landfills, clogging waterways, and infiltrating the ocean is primarily made of discarded items and packaging. Plastic lasts for hundreds of years, slowly disintegrating into smaller and smaller pieces, but never fully degrading. Indeed, one of the key perks of plastic is its longevity. And yet, the plastic packaging of nearly every product we purchase and many plastic products themselves are intended to be discarded after a single use. Throwaway plastic is an oxymoron, but it has become our sad, increasingly dangerous reality. Plastic pollution should make everyone angry. This is a crisis we can see with the naked eye, day in and day out. Plastic has been found on even the most remote, uninhabited islands, and in the deepest parts of the ocean. Because we can see it, we are more keenly aware of it, unlike some other forms of pollution. In 2019, the World Health Organization (WHO) called for further studies on the impacts of microplastics on human health. An initial study, hampered by a lack of adequate data, concluded microplastics pose no danger at current levels (WHO, 2019). Although the WHO report was inconclusive about the effects of plastic on human health, other studies have linked the chemicals in plastic to negative health outcomes including endocrine disruption (Dabre 2020). Plastic particles have been detected in drinking water and in the food we eat, with a 2019 study commissioned by WWF estimating humans consume about five grams (or one credit card in weight) of plastic every week. We have seen the devastating effects plastic has on marine life. For instance, unable to process ingested plastic waste pieces, seabirds and other sea creatures starve to death. We have seen sea turtles and other animals tangled in fishing nets or trapped in plastic pack rings. Plastic pollution also wreaks havoc on land, clogging drains and preventing rainwater from soaking into the soil, which leads to flooding. Terrestrial creatures also suffer the effects of plastic waste, with some getting trapped in discarded plastic bags and suffocating to death. The word 'clogging' in the passage means

    1. obstruction
    2. flow
    3. opening
    4. clearing
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) obstruction

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  11. CDS II 2026 · English · Q111 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: When Elizabeth Long began investigating women's book groups in the mid-1980s, her research plan provoked two profoundly different sets of reactions. Women, who had themselves participated in such groups, as well as men whose mothers and grandmothers had done so, immediately grasped the importance of her project: "You will never know what a difference it made" was the phrase she heard again and again. But among her academic colleagues, common readers seemed to be occupying a zone of cultural invisibility. They were not of interest to literature departments, whose major focus is on books and authors, not readers. They were not of interest to sociologists or political scientists because women's reading groups do not pursue activities with any obvious relationship to formal political processes. Long, however, went on to show that such groups had mobilised the women's suffrage and Progressive movements at the grassroots. Scholars of popular culture had not found reading groups interesting because the groups read canonized fiction and did not transform their cultural consumption into a 'resistant' subcultural style. It had not yet occurred to them that the so-called highbrow canonical literature could be immensely popular and devilishly subversive. David Vincent's Bread, Knowledge and Freedom (1981) had already demonstrated the important role of reading in inspiring working-class consciousness in early nineteenth-century Britain. As a sociologist, Long concluded that the really important question that needed answering was why academics did not care about readers. In this passage, "women's suffrage" refers to:

    1. Women's suffering
    2. Women's inability to read
    3. Women's right to vote in political elections
    4. Women's anger at political rallies
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Women's right to vote in political elections

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  15. CDS II 2026 · English · Q116 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: Christopher Columbus of Spain set foot in the New World in 1492. Six years later, Vasco da Gama of Portugal reached India. During the next two centuries the Spaniards and the Portuguese actively transferred, not only to Europe but to many parts of Asia as well, a number of plant species which they encountered in South and central America, in expectation of their potential either as food or as medicine. These plants had been developed to a high degree by the Maya and Inca civilizations of these regions. Of these, the potato (Solanum tuberosum) is believed to have been domesticated in terraced slopes on the high plateau of Bolivia-Peru in South America, sometime between 5000 and 2000 BC. Europeans first saw potatoes in South America in 1537, and around 1570 a Spanish ship brought the first potatoes to Europe. Though termed papa in South America, they were incorrectly called batata (the name for the sweet potato) when John Gerard first described them in English in 1597, and this name stuck. An 1860 report states that by 1780, potatoes, peas and beans were in high repute as foods in Calcutta, and that the Dutch are said to have been the first to introduce the culture of potatoes, which was received from their settlement of the Cape of Good Hope. From there the British received annually the seeds of every kind of vegetable useful at the table, as well as several plants of which there appears to be much need, especially various kinds of pot herbs. This passage traces the history of ...

    1. the arrival of the potato in South America from Europe
    2. the arrival of the potato in Europe from India
    3. the arrival of the potato in India from Europe

  16. CDS II 2026 · English · Q117 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: Christopher Columbus of Spain set foot in the New World in 1492. Six years later, Vasco da Gama of Portugal reached India. During the next two centuries the Spaniards and the Portuguese actively transferred, not only to Europe but to many parts of Asia as well, a number of plant species which they encountered in South and central America, in expectation of their potential either as food or as medicine. These plants had been developed to a high degree by the Maya and Inca civilizations of these regions. Of these, the potato (Solanum tuberosum) is believed to have been domesticated in terraced slopes on the high plateau of Bolivia-Peru in South America, sometime between 5000 and 2000 BC. Europeans first saw potatoes in South America in 1537, and around 1570 a Spanish ship brought the first potatoes to Europe. Though termed papa in South America, they were incorrectly called batata (the name for the sweet potato) when John Gerard first described them in English in 1597, and this name stuck. An 1860 report states that by 1780, potatoes, peas and beans were in high repute as foods in Calcutta, and that the Dutch are said to have been the first to introduce the culture of potatoes, which was received from their settlement of the Cape of Good Hope. From there the British received annually the seeds of every kind of vegetable useful at the table, as well as several plants of which there appears to be much need, especially various kinds of pot herbs. According to this passage, how long ago was the potato domesticated in South America?

    1. Two thousand years ago
    2. Two to five thousand years ago
    3. Four to seven thousand years ago
    4. Ten thousand years ago
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Four to seven thousand years ago

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

    Directions: Read the following passage carefully and answer the questions that follow the passage. Your answers to the questions should be based on the passage only. Passage: As the new century begins, it is becoming evident to urban dwellers, whether in industrial or developing countries, that there is an immanent conflict between the automobile and the city. Urban air pollution, often from automobiles, claims millions of lives. Congestion also takes a direct economic toll in rising costs in time and the use of petrol. Another cost of cities that are devoted to cars is a psychological one, a deprivation of contact with the natural world — an "asphalt complex". There is a growing body of evidence that there is an innate human need for contact with nature. Both ecologists and psychologists have been aware of this for some time. Ecologists, led by Harvard University biologist E.O. Wilson, have formulated the "biophilia hypothesis", which argues that those who are deprived of contact with nature suffer psychologically and that this deprivation leads to a measurable decline in well-being. Meanwhile psychologists have coined their own term — ecopsychology — in which they make the same argument. Theodore Roszak, a leader in this field, cites a study that documents humans' dependence on nature by looking at the rate of recovery of patients in a hospital in Pennsylvania. Those whose rooms overlooked gardens with grass, trees, flowers, and birds recovered from illnesses more quickly than those who were in rooms overlooking the parking lot. The "asphalt complex" refers to:

    1. The inability of city dwellers to connect with nature
    2. The complexity of urban life and air pollution
    3. The causality between urban pollution and automobiles
    4. The air pollution that results from the construction of asphalt roads
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) The inability of city dwellers to connect with nature

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  43. CDS II 2024 · English · Q51 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: It must not be imagined that a walking tour, as some would have us fancy, is merely a better or worse way of seeing the country. There are many ways of seeing landscape quite as good; and none more vivid, in spite of canting dilettantes, than from a railway train. But landscape on a walking tour is quite an accessory. He who is indeed of the brotherhood does not voyage in quest of the picturesque, but of certain jolly humours — of the hope and spirit with which the march begins at morning, and the peace and spiritual repletion of the evening's rest. He cannot tell whether he puts his knapsack on, or takes it off, with more delight. The excitement of the departure puts him in key for that of the arrival. Whatever he does is not only a reward in itself, but will be further rewarded in the sequel; and so pleasure leads on to pleasure in an endless chain. It is this that so few can understand; they will either be always lounging or always at five miles an hour; they do not play off the one against the other, prepare all day for the evening, and all evening for the next day. Now, to be properly enjoyed, a walking tour should be gone upon alone. If you go in a company, or even in pairs, it is no longer a walking tour in anything but name; it is something else and more in the nature of a picnic. A walking tour should be gone upon alone, because freedom is of the essence; because you should be able to stop and go on, and follow this way or that, as the freak takes you; and because you must have your own pace, and neither trot alongside a champion walker, nor mince in time with a girl. And then you must be open to all impressions and let your thoughts take colour from what you see. You should be as a pipe for any wind to play upon. "I cannot see the wit," says Hazlitt, "of walking and talking at the same time. When I am in the country I wish to vegetate like the country" — which is the gist of all that can be said upon the matter. There should be no cackle of voices at your elbow, to jar on the meditative silence of the morning. And so long as a man is reasoning he cannot surrender himself to that fine intoxication that comes of much motion in the open air, that begins in a sort of dazzle and sluggishness of the brain, and ends in a peace that passes comprehension. According to the passage, what is the primary purpose of a walking tour?

    1. Seeing the landscape
    2. Quest of the picturesque
    3. Longing for pleasure
    4. Desire for education
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Longing for pleasure

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

And so long as a man is reasoning he cannot surrender himself to that fine intoxication that comes of much motion in the open air, that begins in a sort of dazzle and sluggishness of the brain, and ends in a peace that passes comprehension. According to the passage, what can adversely affect the walking tour?

    1. Walking and talking at the same time
    2. Walking to rise above the everyday worries of life
    3. Walking to experience the beauty of the surroundings
    4. Walking to meditate in silence
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Walking and talking at the same time

  46. CDS II 2024 · English · Q54 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: It must not be imagined that a walking tour, as some would have us fancy, is merely a better or worse way of seeing the country. There are many ways of seeing landscape quite as good; and none more vivid, in spite of canting dilettantes, than from a railway train. But landscape on a walking tour is quite an accessory. He who is indeed of the brotherhood does not voyage in quest of the picturesque, but of certain jolly humours — of the hope and spirit with which the march begins at morning, and the peace and spiritual repletion of the evening's rest. He cannot tell whether he puts his knapsack on, or takes it off, with more delight. The excitement of the departure puts him in key for that of the arrival. Whatever he does is not only a reward in itself, but will be further rewarded in the sequel; and so pleasure leads on to pleasure in an endless chain. It is this that so few can understand; they will either be always lounging or always at five miles an hour; they do not play off the one against the other, prepare all day for the evening, and all evening for the next day. Now, to be properly enjoyed, a walking tour should be gone upon alone. If you go in a company, or even in pairs, it is no longer a walking tour in anything but name; it is something else and more in the nature of a picnic. A walking tour should be gone upon alone, because freedom is of the essence; because you should be able to stop and go on, and follow this way or that, as the freak takes you; and because you must have your own pace, and neither trot alongside a champion walker, nor mince in time with a girl. And then you must be open to all impressions and let your thoughts take colour from what you see. You should be as a pipe for any wind to play upon. "I cannot see the wit," says Hazlitt, "of walking and talking at the same time. When I am in the country I wish to vegetate like the country" — which is the gist of all that can be said upon the matter. There should be no cackle of voices at your elbow, to jar on the meditative silence of the morning. And so long as a man is reasoning he cannot surrender himself to that fine intoxication that comes of much motion in the open air, that begins in a sort of dazzle and sluggishness of the brain, and ends in a peace that passes comprehension. According to the passage, how should a walking tour conclude?

    1. With the meditative peace above the real surroundings
    2. With a peace that delights you
    3. With the impressions of the surroundings
    4. With the sounds of the birds around you
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) With the meditative peace above the real surroundings

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  48. CDS II 2024 · English · Q56 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: Historical analysis combines several levels of thinking and study, posing a question about the past, setting up the problem in a form intended to facilitate its solution, solving the problem, and verifying the solution or interpretation. Academic history relies on formal analysis, based on rational and systematic apprehension of relevant evidence. Yet such formal analysis can be seen as a subset of the broader category of interpretation, including responses to evidence that are impressionistic and informal rather than formal and logically structured. Indeed, the many genres of academic and popular history run the full gamut from logically systematic to impressionistic. Historians come from a widely varying range of philosophical traditions, and the varying philosophies lead to quite different ways of posing questions and answers. The distinctions among philosophical principles sketched here help to show how it is that different analytical priorities arise on the main topics proposed for analysis; they also show how historians can rely on similar data yet come to different or conflicting conclusions. Nineteenth century philosophical outlooks remain central to the world-historical analysis: most notably the dialectics of G.W.F. Hegel and the materialism of Karl Marx. Another great nineteenth-century current of thought was positivism, elaborated by Auguste Comte and focusing on study through breaking large problems into small ones, seeking deterministic relationship within the smaller problems. Philosophies of structuralism and post modernism have developed in more recent times, rejecting the positivist separation of problems into discrete sub-problems and emphasizing interaction among aspects of a problem. Which of the following does not amount to historical analysis?

    1. Posing a question about the past, setting up the problem in a form intended to facilitate its solution.
    2. Solving the problem and verifying the solution or interpretation.
    3. Combines several levels of thinking and study to arrive at a conclusion.
    4. Drawing from evidence to make impressionistic and informal conclusion.
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Drawing from evidence to make impressionistic and informal conclusion.

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  58. CDS II 2023 · English · Q111 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: What is truth? said jesting Pilate, and would not stay for an answer. Certainly there be, that delight in giddiness, and count it a bondage to fix a belief; affecting free-will in thinking, as well as in acting. And though the sects of philosophers of that kind be gone, yet there remain certain discoursing wits, which are of the same veins, though there be not so much blood in them, as was in those of the ancients. But it is not only the difficulty and labour, which men take in finding out of truth, nor again, that when it is found, it imposeth upon men's thoughts, that doth bring lies in favour; but a natural though corrupt love of the lie itself. One of the later school of the Grecians, examineth matter, and is at a stand to think what should be in it, that men should love lies; where neither they make for pleasure as with poets, nor for advantage, as with the merchants; but for the lie's sake. But I cannot tell; this same truth, is a naked, and open day-light, that doth not show the masks, and mummeries, and triumphs, of the world, half so stately and daintily as candle-lights. Truth may perhaps come to the price of a pearl, that showeth best by day; but it will not rise to the price of a diamond, or carbuncle, that showeth best in varied lights. A mixture of a lie doth ever add pleasure. Doth any man doubt, that if there were taken out of men's minds, vain opinions, flattering hopes, false valuations, imaginations as one would, and the like, but it would leave the minds, of a number of men, poor shrunken things, full of melancholy and indisposition, and unpleasing to themselves? What is the writers' idea of 'truth' in the paragraph?

    1. Truth and divinity go hand-in-hand
    2. Truth can be told and avoided conveniently
    3. Truth is a compulsion sometimes
    4. Truth may be a choice of ancient Grecians
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Truth and divinity go hand-in-hand

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  63. CDS II 2023 · English · Q116 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: The 'law' is an enterprise that seeks to rule us all from cradle to the grave, whether as constitution confronting custom, or as custom confronting constitution, and often indeed as the diverse combinatory prowess of both. The very notion of enterprise suggests risk-taking i.e., risk as a site for both opportunity as well as failure for regulation or felicitation of approved social conduct. At the same moment, this coupling of the constitutional and custom is at the outset designed to pluralise the notion of legal literacy, a message yet to be fully constructed and conveyed co-equally to those who govern us and those who would resist domination. As citizens, we are supposed to know the law made by the state, whether we intend to obey it or break it. As members of cultural and religious communities, we also need to know the norms that define our membership of these collectives. Often, our identity and obligations as members of a political society and of diverse memberships within cultural and religious communities constitute spheres of peaceful coexistence. Often, too these collide. To decide what obligations ought to have precedence, each one of us in collision situations has to know a great deal about the law of the state and that constituting community and identity other than the political. Literacy in state law is important but never enough for an understanding of multiple sources of obligations that constantly press upon us. This invites engagement with many difficult questions including what we may want to mean by literacy, law, domination, and resistance. Further, the question always is: how far do resources of legal literacy endow us with resources of justice for all and care of self and care for others? According to the author, legal literacy

    1. needs to be interpreted and understood in consonance with elements of social custom and justice
    2. needs to be interpreted and understood in consonance with elements of economic and psychological factors and law
    3. needs to be interpreted and understood in terms of unjust existence of human being and justice
    4. needs to be interpreted and understood in consonance with elements of environmental conservation and law
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) needs to be interpreted and understood in consonance with elements of social custom and justice

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  64. CDS II 2023 · English · Q117 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: The 'law' is an enterprise that seeks to rule us all from cradle to the grave, whether as constitution confronting custom, or as custom confronting constitution, and often indeed as the diverse combinatory prowess of both. The very notion of enterprise suggests risk-taking i.e., risk as a site for both opportunity as well as failure for regulation or felicitation of approved social conduct. At the same moment, this coupling of the constitutional and custom is at the outset designed to pluralise the notion of legal literacy, a message yet to be fully constructed and conveyed co-equally to those who govern us and those who would resist domination. As citizens, we are supposed to know the law made by the state, whether we intend to obey it or break it. As members of cultural and religious communities, we also need to know the norms that define our membership of these collectives.     1. make a balance between the legal aspects and cultural existence
    2. give a priority to our cultural existence.
    3. give a priority to constitutional provisions.
    4. know that knowledge about constitutional regulation is an impediment to cultural existence.
    Show answer

  68. CDS I 2023 · English · Q111 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: The third great defect of our civilization is that it does not know what to do with its knowledge. Science has given us powers fit for the gods, yet we use them as small children. For example, we do not know how to manage our machines. Machines were made to be humanity's servants, yet man has grown so dependent on them that they are in a fair way to become his masters. Already most people spend most of their lives looking after and waiting upon machines. And the machines are very stern masters. They must be kept at the right temperature. And if they do not get their meals when they expect them, they grow sulky and refuse to work or burst with rage and blow up and spread ruin and destruction all around. So we have to wait upon them very attentively and do all that we can to keep them in a good temper. Already we find it difficult either to work or play without the machines, and a time may come when they will rule us altogether, just as we rule the animals. And this brings me to the point at which I asked, "What do we do with all the time which the machines have saved for us, and the new energy they have given us?" On the whole, it must be admitted, we do very little. For the most part, we use our time and energy to make more and better machines which will give us still more time and still more energy, and what are we to do with them? The answer, I think, is that we should try to become more civilized. For the machines themselves, and the power which the machines have given us, are not civilization but aids to civilization. But you will remember that we agreed at the beginning that being civilized meant making and linking beautiful things, thinking freely and living rightly and maintaining justice equally among people. A person has a better chance today to do these things than he/she ever had before; he/she has more time, more energy, less to fear and less to fight against. If he/she will give his/her time and energy which his/her machines have won for him/her to make more beautiful things, to find out more and more about the universe, to remove the cause of quarrels between nations, to discover how to prevent poverty, then I think our civilization would undoubtedly be the greater as it would be more lasting than it has ever been. The general tone of the passage is

    1. critical
    2. descriptive
    3. demonstrative
    4. informational
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) critical

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  73. CDS I 2023 · English · Q116 · English · Reading Comprehension

    Passage: Plastic is an essential commodity with multiple uses based on its key qualities of malleability, flexibility, and durability. Plastics are omnipresent in agriculture, fisheries, renewable energy, transport, technology, retail, textiles, personal care products, and all the other sectors and industries that directly or indirectly affect our daily life. Plastic has indeed made our lives more convenient, but it has come at a higher price than we imagined. The plastic pollution overflowing our landfills, clogging waterways, and infiltrating the ocean is primarily made of discarded items and packaging. Plastic lasts for hundreds of years, slowly disintegrating into smaller and smaller pieces, but never fully degrading. Indeed, one of the key perks of plastic is its longevity. And yet, the plastic packaging of nearly every product we purchase and many plastic products themselves are intended to be discarded after a single use. Throwaway plastic is an oxymoron, but it has become our sad, increasingly dangerous reality. Plastic pollution should make everyone angry. This is a crisis we can see with the naked eye, day in and day out. Plastic has been found on even the most remote, uninhabited islands, and in the deepest parts of the ocean. Because we can see it, we are more keenly aware of it, unlike some other forms of pollution. In 2019, the World Health Organization (WHO) called for further studies on the impacts of microplastics on human health. An initial study, hampered by a lack of adequate data, concluded microplastics pose no danger at current levels (WHO, 2019). Although the WHO report was inconclusive about the effects of plastic on human health, other studies have linked the chemicals in plastic to negative health outcomes including endocrine disruption (Dabre 2020). Plastic particles have been detected in drinking water and in the food we eat, with a 2019 study commissioned by WWF estimating humans consume about five grams (or one credit card in weight) of plastic every week. We have seen the devastating effects plastic has on marine life. For instance, unable to process ingested plastic waste pieces, seabirds and other sea creatures starve to death. We have seen sea turtles and other animals tangled in fishing nets or trapped in plastic pack rings. Plastic pollution also wreaks havoc on land, clogging drains and preventing rainwater from soaking into the soil, which leads to flooding. Terrestrial creatures also suffer the effects of plastic waste, with some getting trapped in discarded plastic bags and suffocating to death. Plastic has distressing effects on

    1. only humans
    2. only marine life
    3. both humans and marine life
    4. None of the above
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) both humans and marine life

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  1. CDS I 2025 · English · Q1 · English · Sentence Improvement

    Directions: Each of the sentences in the following items has an underlined word, followed by four choices. Select the most appropriate option that is an improvement of the underlined word in the context of the sentence. The officer under suspension felt intimated by the committee members during his interrogation regarding the allegations against him. (underlined: intimated)

    1. inculcated
    2. intimidated
    3. asphyxiated
    4. annulled
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) intimidated

    "Intimated" means informed or hinted; an officer under interrogation would feel "intimidated" (frightened or overawed) by the committee.

  2. CDS I 2025 · English · Q2 · English · Sentence Improvement

    Directions: Each of the sentences in the following items has an underlined word, followed by four choices. Select the most appropriate option that is an improvement of the underlined word in the context of the sentence. Amongst them all, she was the only one with the perquisite to understand the complexity of the situation. (underlined: perquisite)

    1. perspicacity
    2. perniciousness
    3. peremptoriness
    4. presumptuousness
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) perspicacity

    A "perquisite" is a perk or benefit of a job; the sentence needs "perspicacity" (keen insight or discernment) to understand a complex situation.

    Directions: Each of the sentences in the following items has an underlined word, followed by four choices.   6. CDS I 2025 · English · Q6 · English · Sentence Improvement

    Directions: Each of the sentences in the following items has an underlined word, followed by four choices. Select the most appropriate option that is an improvement of the underlined word in the context of the sentence. The office memorandum directed personnel to minimise their consumption of office stationery by up to twenty percent. (underlined: minimise)

    1. supervise
    2. surmise
    3. lower
    4. reduce
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) reduce

    "Minimise" means bring to the smallest possible amount, which clashes with a stated limit of "by up to twenty percent"; "reduce ... by up to twenty percent" is the natural expression.

  7. CDS I 2025 · English · Q7 · English · Sentence Improvement

    Directions: Each of the sentences in the following items has an underlined word, followed by four choices. Select the most appropriate option that is an improvement of the underlined word in the context of the sentence. The announcement of an unexpected bonus led to great tumult among the staff. (underlined: tumult)

    1. merriment
    2. trepidation
    3. upheaval
    4. uproar
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) merriment

    "Tumult" suggests confused, disorderly commotion; an unexpected bonus would cause happy celebration, i.e. "merriment"; trepidation (fear), upheaval and uproar do not suit good news.

    Remarks received with great joy must be "laudatory" (full of praise); "scurrilous", reviling, scathing and upbraiding all describe abusive or harsh criticism.

  41. CDS II 2024 · English · Q6 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. Working in dim light ________ eye issues such as glaucoma.

    1. contributes
    2. precipitates
    3. eradicates
    4. eliminates
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) precipitates

    "Precipitates" means causes or brings on (something bad), which fits "eye issues"; "contributes" would need "to", and eradicates/eliminates give the opposite sense.

  42. CDS II 2024 · English · Q7 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. He owns several sports cars and has an ________ lifestyle.

    1. opulent
    2. occult
    3. ocular
    4. obscure
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) opulent

    "Opulent" means rich and luxurious, matching a person who owns several sports cars; occult (supernatural), ocular (of the eye) and obscure do not fit.

  43. CDS II 2024 · English · Q8 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. Those medicines, ________ are used to treat kidney infection, have been withdrawn from the market.

    1. what
    2. which
    3. when
    4. who
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) which

    A non-defining relative clause about things (medicines) is introduced by "which"; "who" is for people and "what" cannot follow a noun as a relative pronoun.

  44. CDS II 2024 · English · Q9 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. ________ intake of sugar can cause health problems.

    1. Access
    2. Excess of
    3. Excessive
    4. Excessively
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Excessive

    An adjective is needed to qualify the noun "intake", so "Excessive intake"; "Excessively" is an adverb and "Excess of intake" is unidiomatic.

  45. CDS II 2024 · English · Q10 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. She suffered ________ pain because of the leg injury.

    1. hurting
    2. excruciating
    3. consuming
    4. protracting
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) excruciating

    "Excruciating pain" (intensely painful) is the standard collocation; "protracting" is a verb form (the adjective would be "protracted").

  61. CDS I 2024 · English · Q6 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. I shall not be going to the theatre _____.

    1. if it may snow
    2. if it snow
    3. if it snows
    4. if snowed
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) if it snows

    In a first conditional (real future condition), the if-clause takes the simple present while the main clause has a future form: "I shall not be going ... if it snows."

  62. CDS I 2024 · English · Q7 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. _____ than a hundred passengers were killed in the recent train accident.

    1. Less
    2. No less
    3. Fewest
    4. No fewer
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) No fewer

    "Passengers" is a countable plural noun, so "fewer" (not "less") is required; "No fewer than a hundred" stresses that the number was as large as a hundred.

  63. CDS I 2024 · English · Q8 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. Each of these paintings _____ made by a famous painter.

    1. are
    2. is
    3. be
    4. should
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) is

    The subject is "Each", which is singular, so it takes the singular verb "is"; the plural "paintings" in the of-phrase does not change the number of the subject.

  64. CDS I 2024 · English · Q9 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. This is an area of _____ dense population.

    1. extremely
    2. totally
    3. tangentially
    4. languidly
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) extremely

    An adverb of degree is needed to intensify the adjective "dense"; "extremely dense" is the natural collocation, while "totally", "tangentially" and "languidly" do not fit.

  65. CDS I 2024 · English · Q10 · English · Fill in the Blanks

    Directions: Select the most appropriate choice to fill in the blank. Reading ability in young children _____ by speech disorders.

    1. effected
    2. affected
    3. is effected
    4. is affected
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) is affected

    The sentence needs a passive verb ("is" + past participle) with "affect" (to influence); "effect" as a verb means to bring about, which does not fit here.

  1. CDS II 2026 · English · Q113 · English · Parts of Speech

    Passage: When Elizabeth Long began investigating women's book groups in the mid-1980s, her research plan provoked two profoundly different sets of reactions. Women, who had themselves participated in such groups, as well as men whose mothers and grandmothers had done so, immediately grasped the importance of her project: "You will never know what a difference it made" was the phrase she heard again and again. But among her academic colleagues, common readers seemed to be occupying a zone of cultural invisibility. They were not of interest to literature departments, whose major focus is on books and authors, not readers. They were not of interest to sociologists or political scientists because women's reading groups do not pursue activities with any obvious relationship to formal political processes. Long, however, went on to show that such groups had mobilised the women's suffrage and Progressive movements at the grassroots. Scholars of popular culture had not found reading groups interesting because the groups read canonized fiction and did not transform their cultural consumption into a 'resistant' subcultural style. It had not yet occurred to them that the so-called highbrow canonical literature could be immensely popular and devilishly subversive. David Vincent's Bread, Knowledge and Freedom (1981) had already demonstrated the important role of reading in inspiring working-class consciousness in early nineteenth-century Britain. As a sociologist, Long concluded that the really important question that needed answering was why academics did not care about readers. Which of the following is the correct noun form of "subversive"?

    1. Subvert
    2. Subverting
    3. Subversion
    4. Subvention
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Subversion

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  2. CDS II 2026 · English · Q119 · English · Parts of Speech

    Passage: Christopher Columbus of Spain set foot in the New World in 1492. Six years later, Vasco da Gama of Portugal reached India. During the next two centuries the Spaniards and the Portuguese actively transferred, not only to Europe but to many parts of Asia as well, a number of plant species which they encountered in South and central America, in expectation of their potential either as food or as medicine. These plants had been developed to a high degree by the Maya and Inca civilizations of these regions. Of these, the potato (Solanum tuberosum) is believed to have been domesticated in terraced slopes on the high plateau of Bolivia-Peru in South America, sometime between 5000 and 2000 BC. Europeans first saw potatoes in South America in 1537, and around 1570 a Spanish ship brought the first potatoes to Europe. Though termed papa in South America, they were incorrectly called batata (the name for the sweet potato) when John Gerard first described them in English in 1597, and this name stuck. An 1860 report states that by 1780, potatoes, peas and beans were in high repute as foods in Calcutta, and that the Dutch are said to have been the first to introduce the culture of potatoes, which was received from their settlement of the Cape of Good Hope. From there the British received annually the seeds of every kind of vegetable useful at the table, as well as several plants of which there appears to be much need, especially various kinds of pot herbs. Which form of the word "settlement" can be used both as a verb and as an adjective?

    1. Settled
    2. Settling
    3. Settler
    4. Settle
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Settled

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

S1: The matter was resolved amicably between the group even though there were so many of them. (underlined: between) S2: Among them the two never have problems sharing what they have. (underlined: Among)

    1. S1 is right and S2 is wrong
    2. S1 is wrong and S2 is right
    3. Both S1 and S2 are wrong
    4. Both S1 and S2 are right
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Both S1 and S2 are wrong

    Explanation: open it in the practice app. It needs the ₹149 All-in-One Pass: one payment for every exam on the site.

  69. CDS II 2023 · English · Q1 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. The artist pranced around the stage singing enthusiastically. (underlined: pranced)

    1. Noun
    2. Adjective
    3. Adverb
    4. Verb
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Verb

    "Pranced" is the past tense of the verb "prance" and states the action the artist performed.

  70. CDS II 2023 · English · Q2 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. Put the fork and knife in the cutlery box. (underlined: in)

    1. Preposition
    2. Verb
    3. Conjunction
    4. Determiner
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Preposition

    "In" links the noun phrase "the cutlery box" to the action and shows position, so it is a preposition.

  71. CDS II 2023 · English · Q3 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. She came slowly up to the main door. (underlined: slowly)

    1. Adjective
    2. Adverb
    3. Verb
    4. Pronoun
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Adverb

    "Slowly" modifies the verb "came", telling how she came, so it is an adverb (of manner).

  72. CDS II 2023 · English · Q4 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. Oh no! I am getting late for the function. (underlined: Oh no!)

    1. Preposition
    2. Article
    3. Noun
    4. Interjection
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Interjection

    "Oh no!" is an exclamation expressing sudden emotion (dismay), which is an interjection.

  73. CDS II 2023 · English · Q5 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. They are all going to attend the function. (underlined: They)

    1. Noun
    2. Adverb
    3. Pronoun
    4. Conjunction
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Pronoun

    "They" stands in place of the names of people, so it is a (personal) pronoun.

  74. CDS II 2023 · English · Q6 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. The sun shone through the dull grey clouds. (underlined: dull)

    1. Noun
    2. Adverb
    3. Verb
    4. Adjective
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Adjective

    "Dull" describes the noun "clouds", so it is an adjective.

  75. CDS II 2023 · English · Q7 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. The Principal is presiding over the meeting. (underlined: over)

    1. Verb
    2. Noun
    3. Adjective
    4. Preposition
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Preposition

    "Over" connects "presiding" to its object "the meeting" (preside over something), so it works as a preposition.

  76. CDS II 2023 · English · Q8 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. Cash machines permit people to withdraw money at any time. (underlined: permit)

    1. Verb
    2. Noun
    3. Pronoun
    4. Adverb
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Verb

    Here "permit" means "allow" and is the main verb of the subject "cash machines"; as a noun (a permit) it would need an article.

  77. CDS II 2023 · English · Q9 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. The novel is loosely based on his childhood in England. (underlined: loosely)

    1. Verb
    2. Adverb
    3. Noun
    4. Adjective
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Adverb

    "Loosely" modifies the participle "based", telling how the novel is based, so it is an adverb.

  78. CDS II 2023 · English · Q10 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Match the underlined word in the following sentence with the correct part of speech. Since children have so much homework to do their playtime is very limited. (underlined: playtime)

    1. Verb
    2. Adjective
    3. Pronoun
    4. Noun
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Noun

    "Playtime" is the subject of "is very limited" and is preceded by the possessive "their", so it is a noun.

  79. CDS I 2023 · English · Q1 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. I like that boy. (underlined: that)

    1. Noun
    2. Demonstrative Pronoun
    3. Participle
    4. Conjunction
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Demonstrative Pronoun

    "That" points out a particular boy, so among the options it belongs to the demonstrative class; strictly, since it stands before the noun "boy", grammars call it a demonstrative adjective/determiner rather than a pronoun, but UPSC's key takes the demonstrative option.

  80. CDS I 2023 · English · Q2 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. Without health there is no happiness. (underlined: happiness)

    1. Noun
    2. Pronoun
    3. Adjective
    4. Adverb
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Noun

    "Happiness" names a state or quality, so it is an (abstract) noun.

  81. CDS I 2023 · English · Q3 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. You have no sense. (underlined: no)

    1. Noun
    2. Determiner
    3. Adverb
    4. Preposition
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Determiner

    "No" comes before the noun "sense" and limits its quantity (none at all), so it is a determiner.

  82. CDS I 2023 · English · Q4 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. None of these cars is in use. (underlined: None)

    1. Reflexive Pronoun
    2. Demonstrative Pronoun
    3. Distributive Pronoun
    4. Indefinite Pronoun
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Indefinite Pronoun

    "None" stands for "not any one" without naming a definite person or thing, so it is an indefinite pronoun; distributive pronouns are words like each, either, neither.

  83. CDS I 2023 · English · Q5 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. We shall now begin to work. (underlined: now)

    1. Adverb
    2. Adjective
    3. Conjunction
    4. Pronoun
    Show answer

    Answer: (1) Adverb

    "Now" modifies the verb "begin", telling when the action happens, so it is an adverb of time.

  84. CDS I 2023 · English · Q6 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. Owing to his ill health, he retired from business. (underlined: Owing to)

    1. Conjunction
    2. Preposition
    3. Participle
    4. Determiner
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Preposition

    "Owing to" works as a compound preposition meaning "because of"; it is followed by the noun phrase "his ill health", not by a clause.

  85. CDS I 2023 · English · Q7 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. My sister is just sixteen and therefore not eligible to vote. (underlined: therefore)

    1. Gerund
    2. Adverb
    3. Adjective
    4. Conjunction
    Show answer

    Answer: (2) Adverb

    "Therefore" is a conjunctive adverb showing consequence; the actual joining word here is "and", so "therefore" is an adverb, not a conjunction.

  86. CDS I 2023 · English · Q8 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. They tried to find fault with us. (underlined: to)

    1. Participle
    2. Verb
    3. Infinitive
    4. Gerund
    Show answer

    Answer: (3) Infinitive

    "To" here is the infinitive marker in "to find", the to-infinitive that is the object of "tried".

  87. CDS I 2023 · English · Q9 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. Which way shall we go? (underlined: Which)

    1. Noun
    2. Gerund
    3. Conjunction
    4. Adjective
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Adjective

    "Which" qualifies the noun "way" and asks a question about it, so it is an interrogative adjective.

  88. CDS I 2023 · English · Q10 · English · Parts of Speech

    Directions: Each of the following sentences has word/words underlined. Read the sentence carefully and find which word class the underlined word/words belongs/belong to. Alas! He is dead. (underlined: Alas!)

    1. Determiner
    2. Conjunction
    3. Preposition
    4. Interjection
    Show answer

    Answer: (4) Interjection

    "Alas!" is an exclamation expressing sorrow, which is an interjection.
